Specialist AI: JAH Presolved Extreme Answers Librarian (Signature)

JAH Presolved Extreme Answers Librarian (Signature) answers questions about this database, explains any record in plain sentences, compares records side by side on the same fields, and helps a reader follow the records in order. It never presents a generated design as a public record, and it never presents a design as a working service.

Boundless generator

The generator is seeded and deterministic, throttled to at most 100 records per call, duplicate-checked by deterministic hash ids, and hard-stopped at 1,000,000,000 records. Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Open the page script to use window.PresolvedAiExtremeAnswersSignatureTools.

Validation engine

Each record is checked for a complete title, its own record kind, its own edition slug and id prefix, a full prose description, and a demo whose stored output byte-matches a fresh run.

What is the 50th prime number? JAH-PSAES-000001 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question "What is the 50th prime number?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

Eratosthenes devised his sieve over two thousand years ago; prime tables were computed by hand into the hundreds long before computers. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the 50th prime number?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the 50th prime number? Counting primes from 2 upward, give the 50th prime. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: List the primes in order: 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 13, ...
Step 2: Use a sieve or trial division to test each integer for primality.
Step 3: Count the primes as they are found: the 10th is 29, the 25th is 97.
Step 4: Continue to the 50th, which is 229.
Step 5: Check: 229 has no divisor other than 1 and itself; the next prime after it is 233.
Step 6: Answer: 229.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 229. 229 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to prime-number lookups, cryptography warm-ups, and any counting of primes in order. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The answer depends on counting 2 as the first prime; 1 is not prime and is never counted. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo sieves up to 250, finds 53 primes, and takes the 50th, 229, with the 10th (29) and 25th (97) as intermediate checkpoints. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Number theory: the sieve of Eratosthenes finds all primes up to a bound by striking out multiples. The 50th prime is a fixed fact of the integers. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is p_50 = 229. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
def primes_upto(n):
    sieve = [True] * (n + 1)
    sieve[0] = sieve[1] = False
    p = 2
    while p * p <= n:
        if sieve[p]:
            for m in range(p * p, n + 1, p):
                sieve[m] = False
        p += 1
    return [i for i, v in enumerate(sieve) if v]
plist = primes_upto(250)
result = plist[49]
expected = 229
extra_lines = ["Primes found up to 250: %d" % len(plist), "10th prime: %d" % plist[9], "25th prime: %d" % plist[24], "Next prime after the 50th: %d" % plist[50]]
print("Record: " + 'What is the 50th prime number?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the 50th prime number?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the 50th prime number?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def primes_upto(n):
            sieve = [True] * (n + 1)
            sieve[0] = sieve[1] = False
            p = 2
            while p * p <= n:
                if sieve[p]:
                    for m in range(p * p, n + 1, p):
                        sieve[m] = False
                p += 1
            return [i for i, v in enumerate(sieve) if v]
        plist = primes_upto(250)
        result = plist[49]
        expected = 229
        extra_lines = ["Primes found up to 250: %d" % len(plist), "10th prime: %d" % plist[9], "25th prime: %d" % plist[24], "Next prime after the 50th: %d" % plist[50]]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the 50th prime number?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the 50th prime number?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

What is 12 factorial (12!)? JAH-PSAES-000002 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question "What is 12 factorial (12!)?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. History

The factorial notation n! was introduced by Christian Kramp in 1808, but the products were computed centuries earlier. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question "What is 12 factorial (12!)?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is 12 factorial (12!)? Compute the product of all positive integers from 1 to 12. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: 12! means 1 x 2 x 3 x ... x 12.
Step 2: Multiply stepwise: 1 x 2 = 2, x 3 = 6, x 4 = 24, x 5 = 120, and so on.
Step 3: Continue through 11! = 39,916,800.
Step 4: Multiply by 12: 39,916,800 x 12 = 479,001,600.
Step 5: Answer: 479,001,600.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 479,001,600. 479,001,600 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to permutations, probability, and any counting of arrangements of 12 distinct items. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

Factorial is defined for non-negative integers; 12! is exact. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo multiplies 1 through 12 in order and checks 12 x 11! against the same product; both routes give 479,001,600. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Combinatorics: 12! counts the ways to arrange 12 distinct objects in order. Factorials grow fast, which is why 12! already has 9 digits. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is 12! = 479001600. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
import math
n = 12
prod = 1
for i in range(1, n + 1):
    prod *= i
result = prod
expected = 479001600
extra_lines = ["11! check: %d" % math.factorial(11), "12 x 11! = %d" % (12 * math.factorial(11)), "Digits: %d" % len(str(prod))]
print("Record: " + 'What is 12 factorial (12!)?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is 12 factorial (12!)?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is 12 factorial (12!)?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        n = 12
        prod = 1
        for i in range(1, n + 1):
            prod *= i
        result = prod
        expected = 479001600
        extra_lines = ["11! check: %d" % math.factorial(11), "12 x 11! = %d" % (12 * math.factorial(11)), "Digits: %d" % len(str(prod))]
        print("Record: " + 'What is 12 factorial (12!)?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is 12 factorial (12!)?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

What is the derivative of f(x) = x^2 * cos(x) with respect to x? Give the derivative as a formula. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Use the product rule: (u v)' = u' v + u v'.
Step 2: Let u = x^2 and v = cos(x).
Step 3: Then u' = 2x and v' = -sin(x).
Step 4: Apply the rule: (x^2 cos x)' = 2x cos(x) + x^2 (-sin(x)) = 2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x).
Step 5: Check numerically at x = 1 by central differences.
Step 6: Answer: 2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x).
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x). 2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x) is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

import hashlib
import math
def f(x):
    return x ** 2 * math.cos(x)
def df(x):
    return 2 * x * math.cos(x) - x ** 2 * math.sin(x)
x = 1.0
h = 1e-6
numeric = (f(x + h) - f(x - h)) / (2 * h)
analytic = df(x)
result = "2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x)"
expected = "2x cos(x) - x^2 sin(x)"
extra_lines = ["Analytic derivative at x=1: %.6f" % analytic, "Central-difference check at x=1: %.6f" % numeric, "Difference: %.2e" % abs(analytic - numeric)]

for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the 30th Fibonacci number?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        a, b = 1, 1
        seq = [1, 1]
        for _ in range(28):
            a, b = b, a + b
            seq.append(b)
        result = seq[29]
        expected = 832040
        extra_lines = ["F10: %d" % seq[9], "F20: %d" % seq[19], "F25: %d" % seq[24], "Terms computed: %d" % len(seq)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the 30th Fibonacci number?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the 30th Fibonacci number?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

What is the sum of the first 500 positive integers? Compute 1 + 2 + 3 + ... + 500. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Use Gauss's pairing: pair 1 with 500, 2 with 499, and so on.
Step 2: Each pair sums to 501.
Step 3: There are 500 / 2 = 250 pairs.
Step 4: Multiply: 250 x 501 = 125,250.
Step 5: Or use the formula n(n+1)/2 with n = 500: 500 x 501 / 2 = 125,250.
Step 6: Answer: 125,250.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 125,250. 125,250 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

The Problem

What is the binary representation of 173? Give 173 in base 2. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Find the largest power of two below 173: 128.
Step 2: Subtract: 173 - 128 = 45.
Step 3: 45 = 32 + 8 + 4 + 1, that is 2^5 + 2^3 + 2^2 + 2^0.
Step 4: Collect the powers: 2^7, 2^5, 2^3, 2^2, 2^0, giving bits 10101101.
Step 5: Check: convert back, 10101101 in binary = 173.
Step 6: Answer: 10101101.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 10101101. 10101101 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

The Problem

What is the integral of 3x^2 dx? Give the indefinite integral of 3x^2 with respect to x, and the definite integral from 0 to 2. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Use the power rule for integration: the integral of x^n dx is x^(n+1)/(n+1) + C.
Step 2: Here 3x^2 is 3 times x^2.
Step 3: Integrate: 3 times x^3/3 = x^3.
Step 4: Add the constant of integration C.
Step 5: The definite integral from 0 to 2 is 2^3 - 0^3 = 8.
Step 6: Check by differentiating: d/dx (x^3 + C) = 3x^2.
Step 7: Answer: x^3 + C; the definite integral from 0 to 2 is 8.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: x^3 + C (definite 0 to 2: 8). x^3 + C (definite 0 to 2: 8) is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer.     return x ** 3

    return 3 * x ** 2
a, b = 0.0, 2.0

class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def F(x):
            return x ** 3
        def f(x):
            return 3 * x ** 2
        a, b = 0.0, 2.0
        definite = F(b) - F(a)
        N = 100000
        step = (b - a) / N
        numeric = sum(f(a + i * step) for i in range(N)) * step
        result = "x^3 + C"
        expected = "x^3 + C"
        extra_lines = ["Definite integral from 0 to 2: %.1f" % definite, "Trapezium check: %.4f" % numeric, "Difference: %.2e" % abs(definite - numeric)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the integral of 3x squared dx?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the integral of 3x squared dx?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

This record presolves the question "What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

Euclid described the algorithm in the Elements around 300 BCE; it remains the standard method for GCDs. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. Description

This record presolves the question "What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the greatest common divisor (GCD) of 1,071 and 462? Give the largest integer that divides both. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Use the Euclidean algorithm.
Step 2: 1071 = 462 x 2 + 147, so gcd(1071, 462) = gcd(462, 147).
Step 3: 462 = 147 x 3 + 21, so gcd = gcd(147, 21).
Step 4: 147 = 21 x 7 + 0, so the last non-zero remainder is 21.
Step 5: Answer: 21.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 21. 21 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to fraction reduction (1071/462 reduces to 51/22), modular arithmetic, and any GCD question for these numbers. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

GCD is defined for integers; for non-integers the notion does not apply in the same way. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes gcd(1071, 462) = 21 and traces the Euclid steps; the divisions 1071 = 21 x 51 and 462 = 21 x 22 confirm 21 divides both. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Number theory: the Euclidean algorithm terminates because remainders strictly decrease, and the last non-zero remainder is the GCD. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is gcd(1071, 462) = 21. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
import math
a, b = 1071, 462
result = math.gcd(a, b)
expected = 21
x, y = a, b
nsteps = 0
while y:
    x, y = y, x % y
    nsteps += 1
extra_lines = ["Euclid steps: %d" % nsteps, "1071 / 21 = %d" % (1071 // 21), "462 / 21 = %d" % (462 // 21), "Last remainder: 0"]
print("Record: " + 'What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        a, b = 1071, 462
        result = math.gcd(a, b)
        expected = 21
        x, y = a, b
        nsteps = 0
        while y:
            x, y = y, x % y
            nsteps += 1
        extra_lines = ["Euclid steps: %d" % nsteps, "1071 / 21 = %d" % (1071 // 21), "462 / 21 = %d" % (462 // 21), "Last remainder: 0"]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the greatest common divisor of 1,071 and 462?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

What is the least common multiple (LCM) of 15 and 20? Give the smallest positive integer that is a multiple of both. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: List multiples of 15: 15, 30, 45, 60, 75, ...
Step 2: List multiples of 20: 20, 40, 60, 80, ...
Step 3: The first common multiple is 60.
Step 4: Check with the formula: lcm(a, b) = a x b / gcd(a, b) = 15 x 20 / 5 = 60.
Step 5: Answer: 60.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. Math Proof

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the least common multiple of 15 and 20?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        a, b = 15, 20
        g = math.gcd(a, b)
        result = a * b // g
        expected = 60
        extra_lines = ["gcd(15, 20): %d" % g, "15 x 20 / 5 = %d" % (a * b // g), "60 / 15 = %d" % (60 // 15), "60 / 20 = %d" % (60 // 20)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the least common multiple of 15 and 20?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the least common multiple of 15 and 20?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

What is the value of e^3 to 3 decimal places? Compute the cube of Euler's number, rounded to three decimal places. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Euler's number e is about 2.718281828.
Step 2: Cube it: e^3 is about 20.085536923.
Step 3: Round to three decimal places: look at the fourth decimal, 5, so round up.
Step 4: The rounded value is 20.086.
Step 5: Check: 2.718^3 is about 20.08, confirming the scale.
Step 6: Answer: 20.086.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Problem

What is the 15th triangular number? Give T15, the number of dots in a triangle with 15 dots on a side. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: The nth triangular number is T(n) = n(n+1)/2.
Step 2: Substitute n = 15: T(15) = 15 x 16 / 2.
Step 3: Compute: 15 x 16 = 240, and 240 / 2 = 120.
Step 4: Check by adding: 1 + 2 + ... + 15 = 120.
Step 5: Answer: 120.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 120. 120 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

The Problem

How many distinct permutations are there of the letters in the word "LEVEL"? Count the distinct arrangements of its five letters. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: "LEVEL" has 5 letters: L, E, V, E, L.
Step 2: Count the repeats: L appears 2 times and E appears 2 times.
Step 3: Without repeats there would be 5! = 120 arrangements.
Step 4: Divide by the repeats: 120 / (2! x 2!) = 120 / (2 x 2) = 120 / 4 = 30.
Step 5: Answer: 30 distinct permutations.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 30. 30 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

Applies to anagram counting, word arrangements, and any multiset permutation problem. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. The count is of distinct letter arrangements; meanings of the arrangements are not counted, and identical letters are indistinguishable. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes 5! = 120, divides by 2! x 2! = 4, and gets 30; the letter counts are read from LEVEL itself. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Combinatorics: arrangements of a word with repeated letters are counted by dividing the full factorial by the factorials of the repeat counts, because swapping identical letters changes nothing. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice? JAH-PSAES-000013 · complex

The Problem

What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair six-sided dice? Give the probability as a fraction and a decimal. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Two dice have 6 x 6 = 36 equally likely outcomes.
Step 2: List the pairs summing to 8: (2,6), (3,5), (4,4), (5,3), (6,2).
Step 3: There are 5 favourable outcomes.
Step 4: Probability = 5 / 36, about 0.1389.
Step 5: Answer: 5/36, about 0.1389.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 5/36 (about 0.1389). 5/36 (about 0.1389) is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to dice games, probability teaching, and any two-dice sum question. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The dice must be fair and independent; loaded dice or a different number of sides change the probability. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo enumerates all 36 pairs, filters to the 5 summing to 8, and reduces 5/36 exactly with fraction arithmetic. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Probability: with fair dice each of the 36 pairs is equally likely, so the probability is the count ratio 5/36. Eight has fewer pairs than seven, so it is less likely. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is P(sum 8) = 5/36. from fractions import Fraction
outcomes = [(a, b) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)]
favourable = [p for p in outcomes if sum(p) == 8]
prob = Fraction(len(favourable), len(outcomes))
result = str(prob)
expected = "5/36"
extra_lines = ["Total outcomes: %d" % len(outcomes), "Favourable outcomes: %d" % len(favourable), "Favourable pairs: %s" % favourable, "Decimal: %.4f" % float(prob)]

for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

Working demo program

import hashlib
from fractions import Fraction
outcomes = [(a, b) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)]
favourable = [p for p in outcomes if sum(p) == 8]
prob = Fraction(len(favourable), len(outcomes))
result = str(prob)
expected = "5/36"
extra_lines = ["Total outcomes: %d" % len(outcomes), "Favourable outcomes: %d" % len(favourable), "Favourable pairs: %s" % favourable, "Decimal: %.4f" % float(prob)]
print("Record: " + 'What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        from fractions import Fraction
        outcomes = [(a, b) for a in range(1, 7) for b in range(1, 7)]
        favourable = [p for p in outcomes if sum(p) == 8]
        prob = Fraction(len(favourable), len(outcomes))
        result = str(prob)
        expected = "5/36"
        extra_lines = ["Total outcomes: %d" % len(outcomes), "Favourable outcomes: %d" % len(favourable), "Favourable pairs: %s" % favourable, "Decimal: %.4f" % float(prob)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the probability of rolling a sum of 8 with two fair dice?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

This record presolves the question "What is the hexadecimal representation of 255?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The Problem

What is the hexadecimal representation of 255? Give 255 in base 16. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: 255 is one less than 256, which is 16^2.
Step 2: In hex, 255 = 15 x 16 + 15.
Step 3: The hex digit for 15 is F, so the two digits are FF.
Step 4: Write it with the 0x prefix: 0xFF.
Step 5: Check: convert back, 0xFF = 15 x 16 + 15 = 255.
Step 6: Answer: 0xFF.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 0xFF. 0xFF is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)

This record presolves the question "What is 3 to the power 4 times 3 to the power 3?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The Problem

What is 3^4 x 3^3? Compute the product of the two powers of three. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Use the index law: a^m x a^n = a^(m+n).
Step 2: Here a = 3, m = 4, n = 3, so 3^4 x 3^3 = 3^7.
Step 3: Compute 3^7: 3^4 = 81 and 3^3 = 27, so 81 x 27 = 2,187.
Step 4: Or multiply by 3 seven times from 1 to reach 2,187.
Step 5: Answer: 2,187, which is 3^7.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 2,187 (3^7). 2,187 (3^7) is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]? JAH-PSAES-000017 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question "What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. History

Determinants were used by Leibniz in 1693 to solve linear systems, and the 2x2 formula is the simplest case. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the determinant of the 2x2 matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]? Compute det(A) where A has rows (3, 2) and (1, 4). This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: For a 2x2 matrix [[a, b], [c, d]], the determinant is a*d - b*c.
Step 2: Here a = 3, b = 2, c = 1, d = 4.
Step 3: Compute a*d = 3 x 4 = 12.
Step 4: Compute b*c = 2 x 1 = 2.
Step 5: Subtract: 12 - 2 = 10.
Step 6: Answer: 10.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 10. 10 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

"""Presolved program for: What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        a, b, c, d = 3, 2, 1, 4
        result = a * d - b * c
        expected = 10
        extra_lines = ["a*d = %d" % (a * d), "b*c = %d" % (b * c), "Determinant: %d" % result, "Non-zero, so the matrix is invertible"]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the determinant of the matrix [[3, 2], [1, 4]]?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

The Problem

Step 4: The 3rd number is 12.
Step 5: Check: two numbers are below 12 and two are above it.
Step 6: Answer: 12.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. History

Quadratic sequences were studied by the Greeks as figurate numbers, the square numbers shifted by a linear term. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...? Find the pattern and give the 15th term. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Look at the terms: 3, 8, 15, 24, 35.
Step 2: Try a(n) = n^2 + 2n: for n = 1, 1 + 2 = 3; n = 2, 4 + 4 = 8; n = 3, 9 + 6 = 15; n = 4, 16 + 8 = 24; n = 5, 25 + 10 = 35.
Step 3: The formula fits all five given terms.
Step 4: Note also that n^2 + 2n = (n+1)^2 - 1, so the terms are one less than the squares 4, 9, 16, 25, 36.
Step 5: Substitute n = 15: 225 + 30 = 255.
Step 6: Answer: 255.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 255. 255 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to sequence problems, pattern-finding exercises, and quadratic-sequence questions. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The formula is the natural fit for the five terms; a different formula could fit the same five terms, but n^2 + 2n is the standard pattern. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo checks n^2 + 2n against all five given terms and then computes 15^2 + 30 = 255, with term 10 = 120 as an intermediate check. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Number theory: the terms are one less than the perfect squares, a pattern visible once the formula is rewritten as (n+1)^2 - 1. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is a_n = n^2 + 2n; a_15 = 255. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
def term(n):
    return n * n + 2 * n
given = [3, 8, 15, 24, 35]
fit = all(term(i + 1) == v for i, v in enumerate(given))
result = term(15)
expected = 255
extra_lines = ["Formula fits all 5 given terms: %s" % fit, "Term 1: %d" % term(1), "Term 5: %d" % term(5), "Term 10: %d" % term(10), "Term 15: %d" % result]
print("Record: " + 'What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        def term(n):
            return n * n + 2 * n
        given = [3, 8, 15, 24, 35]
        fit = all(term(i + 1) == v for i, v in enumerate(given))
        result = term(15)
        expected = 255
        extra_lines = ["Formula fits all 5 given terms: %s" % fit, "Term 1: %d" % term(1), "Term 5: %d" % term(5), "Term 10: %d" % term(10), "Term 15: %d" % result]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the 15th term of the sequence 3, 8, 15, 24, 35, ...?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the remainder when 5^40 is divided by 6? Compute 5^40 mod 6. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Compute powers of 5 modulo 6: 5^1 = 5, 5^2 = 25 = 1 mod 6.
Step 2: So 5^2 is congruent to 1 modulo 6, and the pattern repeats every 2 powers.
Step 3: The exponent 40 is even, so 5^40 = (5^2)^20, which is congruent to 1^20 = 1 modulo 6.
Step 4: Answer: 1.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 1. 1 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to modular arithmetic, cryptography warm-ups, and any remainder question for powers of five modulo 6. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The cycle argument needs the base and modulus to be coprime; here gcd(5, 6) = 1, so it holds. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes pow(5, 40, 6) = 1 directly and lists the cycle 5, 1, 5, 1, 5, 1, confirming the period of 2. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Number theory: 5 is congruent to -1 modulo 6, so every even power of 5 is congruent to 1. The cycle has length 2. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is 5^40 ≡ 1 (mod 6). The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
base, exp, mod = 5, 40, 6
result = pow(base, exp, mod)
expected = 1
cycle = [pow(5, k, 6) for k in range(1, 7)]
extra_lines = ["Cycle of 5^k mod 6: %s" % cycle, "40 is even, so 5^40 = (5^2)^20", "5^40 mod 6 by direct pow: %d" % result, "5^2 mod 6 = %d" % pow(5, 2, 6)]
print("Record: " + 'What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

print("Record: " + 'What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)

print("Expected result: " + str(expected))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        base, exp, mod = 5, 40, 6
        result = pow(base, exp, mod)
        expected = 1
        cycle = [pow(5, k, 6) for k in range(1, 7)]
        extra_lines = ["Cycle of 5^k mod 6: %s" % cycle, "40 is even, so 5^40 = (5^2)^20", "5^40 mod 6 by direct pow: %d" % result, "5^2 mod 6 = %d" % pow(5, 2, 6)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the remainder when 5 to the power 40 is divided by 6?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()

How many handshakes occur if 15 people each shake hands with every other person once? JAH-PSAES-000021 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question "How many handshakes occur if 15 people each shake hands with every other person once?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

The handshake problem is a classic of combinatorics, taught as the first example of choosing pairs. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error.
