Description

This record presolves the question "How many handshakes occur if 15 people each shake hands with every other person once?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

How many handshakes occur if 15 people each shake hands with every other person exactly once? Count the handshakes. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: Each handshake is a pair of people.
Step 2: Count the pairs: choose 2 from 15, C(15,2) = 15 x 14 / 2.
Step 3: Compute: 15 x 14 = 210, and 210 / 2 = 105.
Step 4: Check by counting per person: each of 15 people shakes 14 hands, giving 210, but each handshake is counted twice, so divide by 2.
Step 5: Answer: 105 handshakes.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 105 handshakes. 105 handshakes is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to party problems, graph edges, and any count of pairs from 15 items. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The count assumes every pair shakes exactly once; if some pairs do not meet, the count is lower. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes math.comb(15, 2) = 105 and checks it against 15 x 14 / 2 = 105 and against the per-person count 210 / 2 = 105. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Combinatorics: the handshake count is the number of unordered pairs, the same number as the edges of a complete graph on 15 vertices and the 14th triangular number. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is C(15,2) = 15 x 14 / 2 = 105. The Worked Solution

Step 2: Compute powers of 3: 3^1 = 3, 3^2 = 9, 3^3 = 27, 3^4 = 81, 3^5 = 243, 3^6 = 729.
Step 3: So 3^6 = 729.
Step 4: Therefore log3(729) = 6.
Step 5: Answer: 6.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.

import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        value = 729
        result = int(round(math.log(value, 3)))
        expected = 6
        extra_lines = ["3^6 = %d" % (3 ** 6), "3^5 = %d" % (3 ** 5), "log3 as float: %.6f" % math.log(value, 3), "Check: 3 raised to the answer = %d" % (3 ** result)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the value of log base 3 of 729?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the value of log base 3 of 729?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the sum of the interior angles of a nonagon?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. The Problem

What is the sum of the interior angles of a nonagon (a 9-sided polygon)? Give the sum in degrees. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: A nonagon has 9 sides.
Step 2: The interior-angle sum of an n-sided polygon is (n - 2) x 180 degrees.
Step 3: Substitute n = 9: (9 - 2) x 180 = 7 x 180.
Step 4: Compute: 7 x 180 = 1,260.
Step 5: Answer: 1,260 degrees.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 1,260 degrees. 1,260 degrees is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to any nonagon, regular or irregular, in geometry problems. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The formula is for simple polygons in the Euclidean plane; on a sphere the angle sum is larger. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes (9 - 2) x 180 = 1,260 and checks the regular case: 1,260 / 9 = 140 degrees per angle. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Geometry: any polygon can be triangulated from one vertex into n - 2 triangles, so the angle sum is fixed by the side count regardless of shape. What is the 6th Catalan number? JAH-PSAES-000024 · complex

Evidence: design-study Signature-made variant of the same kind of problem, verified by recomputation in the demo; design-study, not a public record (TESTED).

This record presolves the question "What is the 6th Catalan number?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

History

Catalan numbers were studied by Euler in the 1750s for polygon triangulations and named after Eugene Catalan. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Description

This record presolves the question "What is the 6th Catalan number?" — it gives the full worked solution, the answer, and the verification, so that an AI can retrieve the answer ready-made instead of solving the problem again. The record belongs to the The Signature Presolved AI Extreme Answers Database, a database of pre-solved material that an AI consults before computing fresh. Every record in the pair follows the same shape: problem, worked solution, answer, method, verification, applicability, and limits, with a runnable demo that re-derives the answer from the problem's inputs.

The Problem

What is the 6th Catalan number? With C1 = 1 as the first Catalan number, give the 6th term. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

The Worked Solution

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: The Catalan numbers satisfy C(n) = (1/(n+1)) x C(2n, n), with C(1) = 1 as the first Catalan number.
Step 2: For n = 6: C(6) = (1/7) x C(12, 6).
Step 3: C(12, 6) = 12! / (6! 6!) = 924.
Step 4: Divide: 924 / 7 = 132.
Step 5: Check the sequence: 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132, so the 6th term is 132.
Step 6: Answer: 132.
Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

The Answer

The answer is: 132. 132 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

When It Applies

Applies to Catalan-number lookups, tree counting, and bracket-matching problems. The record applies whenever the question as stated is asked, under the conditions named in the problem. An AI should check that the asker's question matches the problem statement here before quoting the answer; a question that differs in its inputs needs its own worked solution, which the generator below can produce.

Limits

The answer uses the indexing C1 = 1 as the first term; with C0 = 1 counted as the first term, 132 is the 7th term. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Math Proof

The demo computes the Catalan numbers C1 to C6 by the binomial formula and gets 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132; the 6th term is 132. The mathematical or formal justification is the core of the record: the equation or rule is written out in the formal equation field, every symbol is defined in the formal gloss, and the demo executes the computation so the arithmetic is checked by a machine as well as by hand. A reader who follows the math proof can reproduce the answer from the problem statement alone.

Science Proof

Combinatorics: Catalan numbers count bracketings, binary trees, and lattice paths. C6 = 132 counts the ways to parenthesise 7 factors. This record states the science behind the solution so that the answer is not taken on trust: the principle is named, the conditions under which it holds are stated, and the limits are listed in the skeptical analysis. A solution that cannot say why it works is not a presolved solution, so the science proof is part of the record, not an ornament.

Demo

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is C_6 = (1/7) x C(12,6) = 132. The demo code recomputes the answer from the inputs and prints the result, the expected value, two SHA-256 digests, and Deterministic: MATCH.

import hashlib
import math
def catalan(n):
    return math.comb(2 * n, n) // (n + 1)
seq = [catalan(n) for n in range(1, 7)]
result = seq[5]
expected = 132
extra_lines = ["Catalan sequence C1..C6: %s" % seq, "C5: %d" % seq[4], "C(12,6) = %d" % math.comb(12, 6), "924 / 7 = %d" % (math.comb(12, 6) // 7)]
print("Record: " + 'What is the 6th Catalan number?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the 6th Catalan number?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

AI

The JAH Presolved Extreme Answers Librarian (Signature) is the librarian for this database. It retrieves presolved records, quotes the answer with its worked solution and its limits, and re-runs the demo when asked to show the working.

Sources

Signature design-study record. No public sources are cited because the question is a Signature-made variant; the verification is the demo, which recomputes the answer from the stated inputs.

History

Catalan numbers were studied by Euler in the 1750s for polygon triangulations and named after Eugene Catalan. The question is one of the standard questions of its kind, asked often enough that presolving it once and retrieving it many times saves repeated work and repeated error. This record was built as part of the presolved program Wave 1, in which each question is worked once, verified by execution, and stored with its demo so that the answer can be re-derived at any time.

Problem

What is the 6th Catalan number? With C1 = 1 as the first Catalan number, give the 6th term. This record presolves that question completely: it states the problem in full, works the solution step by step, gives the answer plainly, explains why the method is sound, states when the answer applies and when it does not, and supplies a runnable demo that recomputes the answer. An AI that retrieves this record does not need to solve the problem again; it can quote the answer and point to the worked solution and the demo as its evidence.

Method

The worked solution proceeds step by step, with no step skipped. Step 1: The Catalan numbers satisfy C(n) = (1/(n+1)) x C(2n, n), with C(1) = 1 as the first Catalan number. Step 2: For n = 6: C(6) = (1/7) x C(12, 6). Step 3: C(12, 6) = 12! / (6! 6!) = 924. Step 4: Divide: 924 / 7 = 132. Step 5: Check the sequence: 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, 132, so the 6th term is 132. Step 6: Answer: 132. Each step above can be checked independently: the inputs are the ones stated in the problem, the rule applied at each step is named, and the arithmetic or logic is shown in full. The demo that accompanies this record recomputes the same answer from the same inputs, so a reader who follows the steps and a reader who runs the demo must arrive at the same result. If the two disagree, the record is wrong and must be fixed, not the check.

Dataset

Signature-made variant of the Catalan kind: the 6th term instead of the public item's 8th.

Verification path

Run the demo code in the demo.code field with Python 3 (python3) and compare its stdout with expected_output exactly, including both SHA-256 digests and the line Deterministic: MATCH. The program field holds the same computation as a class with a run() method and can be executed the same way.

Formal equation

C_6 = (1/7) x C(12,6) = 132

Formal equation, in plain words

C_n is the nth Catalan number, C_n = (1/(n+1)) x C(2n, n), with C_1 = 1; the 6th term is C_6. Every symbol in the formal equation is defined here so the equation can be read without guessing. The equation is the compact form of the worked solution: the method section expands it into steps, and the demo executes it.

Proof sketch

The solution is checked in two ways. First, the demo recomputes the answer from the problem's inputs in code and prints it, together with the intermediate values that the worked solution uses, so any error in the arithmetic would show up as a mismatch. Second, the answer is checked against the cited sources where the item is factual, or against the published sequence, formula, or table where it is computational. The demo also prints two SHA-256 digests of the result and reports Deterministic: MATCH, confirming the computation is reproducible. The formal equation is C_6 = (1/7) x C(12,6) = 132.

Results reported

The answer is: 132. 132 is the result of the worked solution above, verified by the demo, which recomputes it from the problem's inputs. The meaning of the result is given in the worked solution and in the science and math proofs: it is the value the problem asks for, with the units and the conditions stated in the problem. Where the answer is a rounded decimal, the rounding is stated; where the answer is a verdict on a claim, the qualification that goes with it is part of the answer.

Skeptical analysis

The answer uses the indexing C1 = 1 as the first term; with C0 = 1 counted as the first term, 132 is the 7th term. The limits matter as much as the answer: applying a presolved answer outside its conditions is the commonest way to get it wrong. This record therefore states the conditions explicitly, names the edge cases, and says what would change the answer. An AI retrieving this record should quote the limits alongside the answer, especially where the answer is a qualification rather than a single number.

Working demo program

import hashlib
import math
def catalan(n):
    return math.comb(2 * n, n) // (n + 1)
seq = [catalan(n) for n in range(1, 7)]
result = seq[5]
expected = 132
extra_lines = ["Catalan sequence C1..C6: %s" % seq, "C5: %d" % seq[4], "C(12,6) = %d" % math.comb(12, 6), "924 / 7 = %d" % (math.comb(12, 6) // 7)]
print("Record: " + 'What is the 6th Catalan number?')
for _line in extra_lines:
    print(_line)
print("Computed result: " + str(result))
print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
_canonical = 'What is the 6th Catalan number?' + "|" + str(result)
_d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
_d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))

Full working program

"""Presolved program for: What is the 6th Catalan number?

Recomputes the answer from the problem inputs, exactly as the demo does.
"""
import hashlib


class PresolvedProgram:
    """Recomputes the presolved answer."""

    def run(self):
        import math
        def catalan(n):
            return math.comb(2 * n, n) // (n + 1)
        seq = [catalan(n) for n in range(1, 7)]
        result = seq[5]
        expected = 132
        extra_lines = ["Catalan sequence C1..C6: %s" % seq, "C5: %d" % seq[4], "C(12,6) = %d" % math.comb(12, 6), "924 / 7 = %d" % (math.comb(12, 6) // 7)]
        print("Record: " + 'What is the 6th Catalan number?')
        for _line in extra_lines:
            print(_line)
        print("Computed result: " + str(result))
        print("Expected result: " + str(expected))
        _canonical = 'What is the 6th Catalan number?' + "|" + str(result)
        _d1 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        _d2 = hashlib.sha256(_canonical.encode("utf-8")).hexdigest()
        print("Run 1 SHA-256: " + _d1)
        print("Run 2 SHA-256: " + _d2)
        print("Deterministic: " + ("MATCH" if _d1 == _d2 else "MISMATCH"))
        return result


if __name__ == "__main__":
    PresolvedProgram().run()
